# The category of measurable spaces

Measurable spaces and measurable functions form a (concrete) category .

## Main definitions

* : the functor which sends a measurable space 
  to the space of measures on ; it is a monad (the "Giry monad").

* : sends a topological space  to  equipped with the
  -algebra of Borel sets (the -algebra generated by the open subsets of ).

## Tags

measurable space, giry monad, borel

The category of measurable spaces and measurable functions.

Construct a bundled  from the underlying type and the typeclass.

The underlying measurable space.

is the measurable space of measures over the measurable space . It is the
weakest measurable space, s.t.  is measurable for all measurable sets  in . An
important purpose is to assign a monadic structure on it, the Giry monad. In the Giry monad,
the pure values are the Dirac measure, and the bind operation maps to the integral:
.

In probability theory, the -morphisms  are (sub-)Markov kernels (here  is
the restriction of  to (sub-)probability spaces.)

The Giry monad, i.e. the monadic structure associated with .

An example of an algebra on : the nonnegative Lebesgue integral is a hom, behaving
nicely under the monad operations.

The Borel functor, the canonical embedding of topological spaces into measurable spaces.

# Measurable functions in (pseudo-)metrizable Borel spaces

A limit (over a general filter) of measurable functions valued in a (pseudo) metrizable space is
measurable.

A sequential limit of measurable functions valued in a (pseudo) metrizable space is
measurable.

If the indicator functions of measurable sets  converge to the indicator function of
a set  along a nontrivial countably generated filter, then  is also measurable.

If the indicator functions of a.e.-measurable sets  converge a.e. to the indicator function
of a set  along a nontrivial countably generated filter, then  is also a.e.-measurable.

# The Borel sigma-algebra on Polish spaces

We discuss several results pertaining to the relationship between the topology and the Borel
structure on Polish spaces.

## Main definitions and results

First, we define standard Borel spaces.

* A  is a typeclass for measurable spaces which arise as the Borel sets
  of some Polish topology.

Next, we define the class of analytic sets and establish its basic properties.

* : a set in a topological space is analytic if it is the continuous
  image of a Polish space. Equivalently, it is empty, or the image of .
* : a continuous image of an analytic set is
  analytic.
* : in a Polish space, any Borel-measurable set is analytic.

Then, we show Lusin's theorem that two disjoint analytic sets can be separated by Borel sets.

*  states that there exists a measurable set containing  and disjoint
  from .
*  shows that two disjoint analytic sets are separated by a
  Borel set.

We then prove the Lusin-Souslin theorem that a continuous injective image of a Borel subset of
a Polish space is Borel. The proof of this nontrivial result relies on the above results on
analytic sets.

*  asserts that, if  is a Borel measurable set in
  a Polish space, then the image of  under a continuous injective map is still Borel measurable.
*  states that a continuous injective map on a Polish space
  is a measurable embedding for the Borel sigma-algebra.
*  is the same result for a map restricted to a measurable set
  on which it is continuous.
*  states that a measurable injective map from
  a standard Borel space to a second-countable topological space is a measurable embedding.
* : in a Polish space, a set is clopenable (i.e., it can be made
  open and closed by using a finer Polish topology) if and only if it is Borel-measurable.

We use this to prove several versions of the Borel isomorphism theorem.

*  : Any two uncountable standard Borel spaces
  are Borel isomorphic.
*  : Any two standard Borel spaces of the same cardinality
  are Borel isomorphic.

### Standard Borel Spaces

A standard Borel space is a measurable space arising as the Borel sets of some Polish topology.
This is useful in situations where a space has no natural topology or
the natural topology in a space is non-Polish.

a compatible Polish topology.

### Analytic sets

An analytic set is a set which is the continuous image of some Polish space. There are several
equivalent characterizations of this definition. For the definition, we pick one that avoids
universe issues: a set is analytic if and only if it is a continuous image of  (or if it
is empty). The above more usual characterization is given
in .

Warning: these are analytic sets in the context of descriptive set theory (which is why they are
registered in the namespace ). They have nothing to do with analytic sets in the
context of complex analysis.

The image of an open set under a continuous map is analytic.

A set is analytic if and only if it is the continuous image of some Polish space.

The continuous image of an analytic set is analytic

A countable intersection of analytic sets is analytic.

For the proof, write each  as the continuous image under a map  of a
   Polish space . The product space  is also Polish, and so is the subset
    of sequences  for which  is independent of . The set  is Polish, and
   the range of  on  is exactly , so this set is analytic.

A countable union of analytic sets is analytic.

For the proof, write each  as the continuous image under a map  of a
   Polish space . The union space  is also Polish, and the map  which
   coincides with  on  sends it to .

Given a Borel-measurable set in a Polish space, there exists a finer Polish topology making
it clopen. This is in fact an equivalence, see .

A Borel-measurable set in a Polish space is analytic.

For a short proof (avoiding measurable induction), one sees  as a closed set for a finer
   topology . It is analytic for this topology. As the identity from  to  is continuous
   and the image of an analytic set is analytic, it follows that  is also analytic for .

Given a Borel-measurable function from a Polish space to a second-countable space, there exists
a finer Polish topology on the source space for which the function is continuous.

The image of a measurable set in a standard Borel space under a measurable map
is an analytic set.

Preimage of an analytic set is an analytic set.

### Separating sets with measurable sets

Two sets  and  in a measurable space are measurably separable if there
exists a measurable set containing  and disjoint from .
This is mostly interesting for Borel-separable sets.

The hard part of the Lusin separation theorem saying that two disjoint analytic sets are
contained in disjoint Borel sets (see the full statement in ).
Here, we prove this when our analytic sets are the ranges of functions from .

We follow Kechris, *Classical Descriptive Set Theory* (Theorem 14.7).
   If the ranges are not Borel-separated, then one can find two cylinders of length one whose
   images are not Borel-separated, and then two smaller cylinders of length two whose images are
   not Borel-separated, and so on. One thus gets two sequences of cylinders, that decrease to two
   points  and . Their images are different by the disjointness assumption, hence contained
   in two disjoint open sets by the T2 property. By continuity, long enough cylinders around 
   and  have images which are separated by these two disjoint open sets, a contradiction.

consider the set of pairs of cylinders of some length whose images are not Borel-separated

for each such pair, one can find longer cylinders whose images are not Borel-separated either

construct inductively decreasing sequences of cylinders whose images are not separated

check that at the -th step we deal with cylinders of length

check that the cylinders we construct are indeed decreasing, by checking that the coordinates
  are stationary.

denote by  and  the limit points of these two sequences of cylinders.

by design, the cylinders around these points have images which are not Borel-separable.

consider two open sets separating  and .

for large enough , these open sets separate the images of long cylinders around  and

this is a contradiction.

The **Lusin separation theorem**: if two analytic sets are disjoint, then they are contained in
disjoint Borel sets.

**Suslin's Theorem**: in a Hausdorff topological space, an analytic set with an analytic
complement is measurable.

### Measurability of preimages under measurable maps

If  is a surjective Borel measurable map from a standard Borel space
to a countably separated measurable space, then the preimage of a set 
is measurable if and only if the set is measurable.
One implication is the definition of measurability, the other one heavily relies on  being a
standard Borel space.

### The Borel Isomorphism Theorem

If two standard Borel spaces admit Borel measurable injections to one another,
then they are Borel isomorphic.

Any uncountable standard Borel space is Borel isomorphic to the Cantor space .

Borel isomorphic.

they are Borel isomorphic.

# Results about strongly measurable functions

In measure theory it is often assumed that some space is a , i.e. a separable and
completely metrizable topological space, because it ensures a nice interaction between the topology
and the measurable space structure. Moreover a strongly measurable function whose codomain is a
metric space is measurable and has a separable range
(see ). Therefore if the codomain is also complete,
by corestricting the function to the closure of its range, some results about measurable functions
can be extended to strongly measurable functions without assuming separability on the codomain.
The purpose of this file is to collect those results.

The infinite product of strongly measurable functions is measurable,  version.

The infinite sum of strongly measurable functions is measurable,  version.

The infinite product of strongly measurable functions is measurable.

The infinite sum of strongly measurable functions is measurable.

The product of almost everywhere strongly measurable functions is measurable.

The sum of almost everywhere strongly measurable functions is measurable.

The product of strongly measurable functions is measurable.

The sum of strongly measurable functions is measurable.

The product of almost everywhere strongly measurable functions is measurable.

The sum of almost everywhere strongly measurable functions is measurable.

# Besicovitch covering theorems

The topological Besicovitch covering theorem ensures that, in a nice metric space, there exists a
number  such that, from any family of balls with bounded radii, one can extract  families,
each made of disjoint balls, covering together all the centers of the initial family.

By "nice metric space", we mean a technical property stated as follows: there exists no satellite
configuration of  points (with a given parameter ). Such a configuration is a family
of  balls, where the first  balls all intersect the last one, but none of them contains
the center of another one and their radii are controlled. This property is for instance
satisfied by finite-dimensional real vector spaces.

In this file, we prove the topological Besicovitch covering theorem,
in .

The measurable Besicovitch theorem ensures that, in the same class of metric spaces, if at every
point one considers a class of balls of arbitrarily small radii, called admissible balls, then
one can cover almost all the space by a family of disjoint admissible balls.
It is deduced from the topological Besicovitch theorem, and proved
in .

This implies that balls of small radius form a Vitali family in such spaces. Therefore, theorems
on differentiation of measures hold as a consequence of general results. We restate them in this
context to make them more easily usable.

## Main definitions and results

*  is the type of all satellite configurations of  points
  in the metric space , with parameter .
*  is a class recording that there exist  and  such that
  there is no satellite configuration of  points with parameter .
*  is the topological Besicovitch covering theorem: from any
  family of balls one can extract finitely many disjoint subfamilies covering the same set.
*  is the measurable Besicovitch covering theorem: from any
  family of balls with arbitrarily small radii at every point, one can extract countably many
  disjoint balls covering almost all the space. While the value of  is relevant for the precise
  statement of the topological Besicovitch theorem, it becomes irrelevant for the measurable one.
  Therefore, this statement is expressed using the -valued
  typeclass .

We also restate the following specialized versions of general theorems on differentiation of
measures:
*  ensures that  tends
  almost surely to the Radon-Nikodym derivative of  with respect to  at .
*  states that almost every point in an arbitrary set 
  is a Lebesgue density point, i.e.,  tends to  as
   tends to . A stronger version for measurable sets is given in
  .

## Implementation

#### Sketch of proof of the topological Besicovitch theorem:

We choose balls in a greedy way. First choose a ball with maximal radius (or rather, since there
is no guarantee the maximal radius is realized, a ball with radius within a factor  of the
supremum). Then, remove all balls whose center is covered by the first ball, and choose among the
remaining ones a ball with radius close to maximum. Go on forever until there is no available
center (this is a transfinite induction in general).

Then define inductively a coloring of the balls. A ball will be of color  if it intersects
already chosen balls of color , ..., , but none of color . In this way, balls of the
same color form a disjoint family, and the space is covered by the families of the different colors.

The nontrivial part is to show that at most  colors are used. If one needs  colors,
consider the first time this happens. Then the corresponding ball intersects  balls of the
different colors. Moreover, the inductive construction ensures that the radii of all the balls are
controlled: they form a satellite configuration with  balls (essentially by definition of
satellite configurations). Since we assume that there are no such configurations, this is a
contradiction.

#### Sketch of proof of the measurable Besicovitch theorem:

From the topological Besicovitch theorem, one can find a disjoint countable family of balls
covering a proportion  of the space. Taking a large enough finite subset of these
balls, one gets the same property for finitely many balls. Their union is closed. Therefore, any
point in the complement has around it an admissible ball not intersecting these finitely many balls.
Applying again the topological Besicovitch theorem, one extracts from these a disjoint countable
subfamily covering a proportion  of the remaining points, and then even a disjoint
finite subfamily. Then one goes on again and again, covering at each step a positive proportion of
the remaining points, while remaining disjoint from the already chosen balls. The union of all these
balls is the desired almost everywhere covering.

### Satellite configurations


A satellite configuration is a configuration of  points that shows up in the inductive
construction for the Besicovitch covering theorem. It depends on some parameter .

This is a family of balls (indexed by , with center  and radius ) such
that the last ball intersects all the other balls (condition ),
and given any two balls there is an order between them, ensuring that the first ball does not
contain the center of the other one, and the radius of the second ball cannot be larger than
the radius of the first ball (up to a factor ). This order corresponds to the order of choice
in the inductive construction: otherwise, the second ball would have been chosen before.
This is the condition .

Finally, the last ball is chosen after all the other ones, meaning that  can be strengthened
by keeping only one side of the alternative in .

### Consequences on differentiation of measures

In a space with the Besicovitch covering property, the set of closed balls with positive radius
forms a Vitali family. This is essentially a restatement of the measurable Besicovitch theorem.

The main feature of the Besicovitch Vitali family is that its filter at a point  corresponds
to convergence along closed balls. We record one of the two implications here, which will enable us
to deduce specific statements on differentiation of measures in this context from the general
versions.

In a space with the Besicovitch covering property, the ratio of the measure of balls converges
almost surely to the Radon-Nikodym derivative.

# Satellite configurations for Besicovitch covering lemma in vector spaces

The Besicovitch covering theorem ensures that, in a nice metric space, there exists a number 
such that, from any family of balls with bounded radii, one can extract  families, each made of
disjoint balls, covering together all the centers of the initial family.

A key tool in the proof of this theorem is the notion of a satellite configuration, i.e., a family
of  balls, where the first  balls all intersect the last one, but none of them contains
the center of another one and their radii are controlled. This is a technical notion, but it shows
up naturally in the proof of the Besicovitch theorem (which goes through a greedy algorithm): to
ensure that in the end one needs at most  families of balls, the crucial property of the
underlying metric space is that there should be no satellite configuration of  points.

This file is devoted to the study of this property in vector spaces: we prove the main result
of Füredi and Loeb, On the best constant for the Besicovitch covering theorem,
which shows that the optimal such  in a vector space coincides with the maximal number
of points one can put inside the unit ball of radius  under the condition that their distances
are bounded below by .
In particular, this number is bounded by  by a straightforward measure argument.

## Main definitions and results

*  is the maximal number of points one can put inside the unit ball
  of radius  in the vector space , under the condition that their distances
  are bounded below by .
*  shows that .
*  is a constant , but close enough to  that satellite configurations
  with this parameter  are not worst than for .
*  is the main theorem, saying that there are
  no satellite configurations of  points, for the parameter .

# Uniformly locally doubling measures and Lebesgue's density theorem

Lebesgue's density theorem states that given a set  in a sigma compact metric space with
locally-finite uniformly locally doubling measure  then for almost all points  in , for any
sequence of closed balls  containing , the limit  as
.

In this file we combine general results about existence of Vitali families for uniformly locally
doubling measures with results about differentiation along a Vitali family to obtain an explicit
form of Lebesgue's density theorem.

* : a version of Lebesgue's density
  theorem for sequences of balls converging on a point but whose centres are not required to be
  fixed.

at  contain all  when .

the Vitali covering theorem gives a family that works well at small scales, thanks to the
   doubling property. We enlarge this family to add large sets, to make sure that all balls and not
   only small ones belong to the family, for convenience.

contain all balls  when .

The measure is doubling on scales smaller than . Therefore, we treat differently small
   and large balls. For large balls, this follows directly from the enlargement we used in the
   definition.

For small balls, there is the difficulty that  could be large but still the ball could be
   small, if the annulus  is empty. We split between the cases 
   and , and use the doubling for the former and rough estimates for the latter.

not required to be fixed.

# Differentiation of measures

On a second countable metric space with a measure , consider a Vitali family (i.e., for each 
one has a family of sets shrinking to , with a good behavior with respect to covering theorems).
Consider also another measure . Then, for almost every , the ratio  converges when
 shrinks to  along the Vitali family, towards the Radon-Nikodym derivative of  with
respect to . This is the main theorem on differentiation of measures.

This theorem is proved in this file, under the name . Note that,
almost surely,  is eventually positive and finite (see
 and ), so the
ratio really makes sense.

For concrete applications, one needs concrete instances of Vitali families, as provided for instance
by  (for balls) or by  (for doubling measures).

Specific applications to Lebesgue density points and the Lebesgue differentiation theorem are also
derived:

*  states that, for almost every point , then the

## Sketch of proof

Let  be a Vitali family for . Assume for simplicity that  is absolutely continuous with
respect to , as the case of a singular measure is easier.

It is easy to see that a set  on which  satisfies , by using
a disjoint subcovering provided by the definition of Vitali families. Similarly for the limsup.
It follows that a set on which  oscillates has measure , and therefore that
 converges almost surely (). Moreover, on a set where the
limit is close to a constant , one gets , using again a covering lemma as above.
It follows that  is equal to , where  is the
limit of  at  (which is well defined almost everywhere). By uniqueness of the
Radon-Nikodym derivative, one gets  almost everywhere, completing
the proof.

There is a difficulty in this sketch: this argument works well when  is measurable,
but there is no guarantee that this is the case, especially if one doesn't make further assumptions
on the Vitali family. We use an indirect argument to show that  is always
almost everywhere measurable, again based on the disjoint subcovering argument
(see ), and then proceed as sketched above
but replacing  by a measurable version called .

## Counterexample

The standing assumption in this file is that spaces are second countable. Without this assumption,
measures may be zero locally but nonzero globally, which is not compatible with differentiation
theory (which deduces global information from local one). Here is an example displaying this
behavior.

Define a measure  by  if  is covered by countably many balls of radius ,
and  otherwise. This is indeed a countably additive measure, which is moreover
locally finite and doubling at small scales. It vanishes on every ball of radius , so all the
quantities in differentiation theory (defined as ratios of measures as the radius tends to zero)
make no sense. However, the measure is not globally zero if the space is big enough.

# Liminf, limsup, and uniformly locally doubling measures.

This file is a place to collect lemmas about liminf and limsup for subsets of a metric space
carrying a uniformly locally doubling measure.

## Main results:

* : the limsup of the closed thickening of a sequence of subsets
  of a metric space is unchanged almost everywhere for a uniformly locally doubling measure if the
  sequence of distances is multiplied by a positive scale factor. This is a generalisation of a
  result of Cassels, appearing as Lemma 9 on page 217 of
  J.W.S. Cassels, *Some metrical theorems in Diophantine approximation. I*.

  rather than closed thickenings.

# Covering theorems for Lebesgue measure in one dimension

We have a general theory of covering theorems for doubling measures, developed notably
in . In this file, we expand the API for this theory in one dimension,
by showing that intervals belong to the relevant Vitali family.

# Vitali covering theorems

The topological Vitali covering theorem, in its most classical version, states the following.
Consider a family of balls  in a metric space, with uniformly bounded
radii. Then one can extract a disjoint subfamily indexed by , such that any 
is included in a ball .

We prove this theorem in .
It is deduced from a more general version, called
, which applies to any family of sets
together with a size function  (think "radius" or "diameter").

We deduce the measurable Vitali covering theorem. Assume one is given a family  of closed sets
with nonempty interior, such that each  is included in a ball  and covers a
definite proportion of the ball  for a given measure  (think of the situation
where  is a doubling measure and  is a family of balls). Consider a set  at which the
family is fine, i.e., every point of  belongs to arbitrarily small elements of . Then one
can extract from  a disjoint subfamily that covers almost all . It is proved in
.

A way to restate this theorem is to say that the set of closed sets  with nonempty interior
covering a fixed proportion  of the ball  forms a Vitali family.
This version is given in .

subfamily  such that the -enlargement of this family covers all elements of , where 
is any fixed number.

element  of  of size larger than that of  up to , i.e., .

We state the lemma slightly more generally, with an indexed family of sets  for , for
wider applicability.

The proof could be formulated as a transfinite induction. First pick an element of  with 
 as large as possible (up to a factor of ). Then among the remaining elements not intersecting
 the already chosen one, pick another element with large . Go on forever (transfinitely) until
 there is nothing left.

 Instead, we give a direct Zorn-based argument. Consider a maximal family  of disjoint sets
 with the following property: if an element  of  intersects some element  of , then it
 intersects some  with . Such a maximal family exists by Zorn. If this

 intersect any element of , with  almost as large as possible. One checks easily
 that  still has this property, contradicting the maximality. Therefore, 
 intersects all elements of , and by definition it satisfies all the desired properties.

# Vitali families

On a metric space  with a measure , consider for each  a family of measurable sets with
nonempty interiors, called . This family is a Vitali family if it satisfies the following
property: consider a (possibly non-measurable) set , and for any  in  a
subfamily  of  containing sets of arbitrarily small diameter. Then one can extract
a disjoint subfamily covering almost all .

Vitali families are provided by covering theorems such as the Besicovitch covering theorem or the
Vitali covering theorem. They make it possible to formulate general versions of theorems on
differentiations of measure that apply in both contexts.

This file gives the basic definition of Vitali families. More interesting developments of this
notion are deferred to other files:
* constructions of specific Vitali families are provided by the Besicovitch covering theorem, in
  , and by the Vitali covering theorem, in .
* The main theorem on differentiation of measures along a Vitali family is proved in
  .

## Main definitions

*  is a structure made, for each , of a family of sets around , such that
  one can extract an almost everywhere disjoint covering from any subfamily containing sets of
  arbitrarily small diameters.

Let  be such a Vitali family.
*  describes the subfamilies of  from which one can extract almost
  everywhere disjoint coverings. This property, called
  , is essentially a restatement of the definition
  of a Vitali family. We also provide an API to use efficiently such a disjoint covering.
*  is a filter on sets of , such that convergence with respect to this filter
  means convergence when sets in the Vitali family shrink towards .

## References

* Herbert Federer, Geometric Measure Theory, Chapter 2.8
  (Vitali families are called Vitali relations there)

# Almost everywhere equal functions

We build a space of equivalence classes of functions, where two functions are treated as identical
if they are almost everywhere equal. We form the set of equivalence classes under the relation of
being almost everywhere equal, which is sometimes known as the  space.
To use this space as a basis for the  spaces and for the Bochner integral, we consider
equivalence classes of strongly measurable functions (or, equivalently, of almost everywhere
strongly measurable functions.)

See  for  space.

# From equality of integrals to equality of functions

This file provides various statements of the general form "if two functions have the same integral
on all sets, then they are equal almost everywhere".
The different lemmas use various hypotheses on the class of functions, on the target space or on the
possible finiteness of the measure.

This file is about Bochner integrals. See the file  for Lebesgue integrals.

## Main statements

All results listed below apply to two functions , together with two main hypotheses,

* Define the conditional expectation of an  function, as an element of . This is the
  orthogonal projection on the subspace of almost everywhere -measurable functions.
* Show that the conditional expectation of the indicator of a measurable set with finite measure
  is integrable and define a map  which to a set associates a linear
  map. That linear map sends  to the conditional expectation of the indicator of the set
  with value .
* Extend that map to . This is done using the same
  construction as the Bochner integral (see the file ).
* Define the conditional expectation of a function , which is an integrable function
   equal to 0 if  is not integrable, and equal to an -measurable representative of
   applied to , the equivalence class of  in .

performed in this file.

# Approximation in Lᵖ by continuous functions

This file proves that bounded continuous functions are dense in , for , if the
domain  of the functions is a normal topological space and the measure  is weakly regular.
It also proves the same results for approximation by continuous functions with compact support
when the space is locally compact and  is regular.

The result is presented in several versions. First concrete versions giving an approximation
up to  in these various contexts, and then abstract versions stating that the topological
closure of the relevant subgroups of  are the whole space.

*  states that, in a locally compact
  space, an  function can be approximated by continuous functions with compact support,
  in the sense that  is small.
* : same result, but expressed in
  terms of .

Versions with  instead of  are specialized to the case .
Versions with  instead of  drop the locally
compact assumption and give only approximation by a bounded continuous function.

* : The subgroup
   of , the additive subgroup of
   consisting of equivalence classes containing a continuous representative, is dense in
  .
* : For finite-measure , the continuous linear
  map  from  to  has dense range.
* : For compact  and finite-measure , the continuous linear
  map  from  to  has dense range.

Note that for  this result is not true:  the characteristic function of the set  in
 cannot be continuously approximated in .

The proof is in three steps.  First, since simple functions are dense in , it suffices to prove
the result for a scalar multiple of a characteristic function of a measurable set . Secondly,
since the measure  is weakly regular, the set  can be approximated above by an open set and
below by a closed set.  Finally, since the domain  is normal, we use Urysohn's lemma to find a
continuous function interpolating between these two sets.

# Convergence in measure

We define convergence in measure which is one of the many notions of convergence in probability.
A sequence of functions  is said to converge in measure to some function 
if for all , the measure of the set  tends to 0 as 
converges along some given filter .

Convergence in measure is most notably used in the formulation of the weak law of large numbers
and is also useful in theorems such as the Vitali convergence theorem. This file provides some
basic lemmas for working with convergence in measure and establishes some relations between
convergence in measure and other notions of convergence.

## Main definitions

* :  converges
  in -measure to .

## Main results

* : convergence almost everywhere in a finite
  measure space implies convergence in measure.
* : if  is a sequence of functions
  which converges in measure to , then  has a subsequence which converges almost
  everywhere to .
* : for a sequence of functions ,
  convergence in measure is equivalent to the fact that every subsequence has another subsequence
  that converges almost surely.
* : convergence in Lp implies convergence
  in measure.

A sequence of functions  is said to converge in measure to some function  if for all
, the measure of the set  tends to 0 as  converges along
some given filter .

# Egorov theorem

This file contains the Egorov theorem which states that an almost everywhere convergent
sequence on a finite measure space converges uniformly except on an arbitrarily small set.
This theorem is useful for the Vitali convergence theorem as well as theorems regarding
convergence in measure.

## Main results

* : Egorov's theorem which shows that a sequence of
  almost everywhere convergent functions converges uniformly except on an arbitrarily small set.

Given a sequence of functions  and a function ,  is the
set of elements such that  and  are separated by at least  for some
.

This definition is useful for Egorov's theorem.

Given some ,  provides the index such that
 (intersected with a set of finite measure) has measure less than
.

This definition is useful for Egorov's theorem.

Given some ,  is the union of  with
specific indices such that  has measure less equal than .

This definition is useful for Egorov's theorem.

**Egorov's theorem**: If  is a sequence of functions that
converges to  almost everywhere on a measurable set  of finite measure,
and the distance between  and  is measurable for all ,
then for all , there exists a subset  such that  and  converges to 
uniformly on . We require the index type  to be countable, and usually .

In other words, a sequence of almost everywhere convergent functions converges uniformly except on
an arbitrarily small set.

**Egorov's theorem**: If  is a sequence of strongly measurable functions that
converges to  almost everywhere on a measurable set  of finite measure,
then for all , there exists a subset  such that  and  converges to 
uniformly on . We require the index type  to be countable, and usually .

In other words, a sequence of almost everywhere convergent functions converges uniformly except on
an arbitrarily small set.

Egorov's theorem for finite measure spaces.
Version with measurable distances.

# Bergelson's intersectivity lemma

This file proves the Bergelson intersectivity lemma: In a finite measure space, a sequence of events
that have measure at least  has an infinite subset whose finite intersections all have positive
volume.

This is in some sense a finitary version of the second Borel-Cantelli lemma.

## Implementation

Typical versions of these results in the literature have much stronger assumptions:  would
typically be open, and the derivative  would depend continuously on  and be invertible
everywhere, to have the local inverse theorem at our disposal. The proof strategy under our weaker
assumptions is more involved. We follow Fremlin, *Measure Theory* (volume 2).

The first remark is that, if  is sufficiently well approximated by a linear map  on a set
, then  expands the volume of  by at least  and at most , where
the closeness condition depends on  in a non-explicit way (see 
and ). This fact holds for balls by a simple inclusion argument,
and follows for general sets using the Besicovitch covering theorem to cover the set by balls with
measures adding up essentially to .

When  is differentiable on , one may partition  into countably many subsets 
(where  is measurable), on each of which  is well approximated by a linear map, so that the
above results apply. See , which
follows from the pointwise differentiability (in a non-completely trivial way, as one should ensure
a form of uniformity on the sets of the partition).

Combining the above two results would give the conclusion, except for two difficulties: it is not
obvious why  and  should be measurable, which prevents us from using countable
additivity for the measure and the integral. It turns out that  is indeed measurable,
and that  is almost everywhere measurable, which is enough to recover countable additivity.

The measurability of  follows from the deep Lusin-Souslin theorem ensuring that, in a
Polish space, a continuous injective image of a measurable set is measurable.

The key point to check the almost everywhere measurability of  is that, if  is approximated
up to  by a linear map on a set , then  is within  of  on a full measure subset
of  (namely, its density points). With the above approximation argument, it follows that 
is the almost everywhere limit of a sequence of measurable functions (which are constant on the
pieces of the good discretization), and is therefore almost everywhere measurable.

### Measure zero of the image, over non-measurable sets

If a set has measure , then its image under a differentiable map has measure zero. This doesn't
require the set to be measurable. In the same way, if  is differentiable on a set  with
non-invertible derivative everywhere, then  has measure , again without measurability
assumptions.


A differentiable function maps sets of measure zero to sets of measure zero.

A version of **Sard's lemma** in fixed dimension: given a differentiable function from 
to  and a set where the differential is not invertible, then the image of this set has
zero measure. Here, we give an auxiliary statement towards this result.

A version of Sard lemma in fixed dimension: given a differentiable function from  to  and
a set where the differential is not invertible, then the image of this set has zero measure.

### Weak measurability statements

We show that the derivative of a function on a set is almost everywhere measurable, and that the
image  is measurable if  is injective on . The latter statement follows from the
Lusin-Souslin theorem.

with respect to Lebesgue measure. Note that, in general, it is not genuinely measurable there,
as  is not unique (but only on a set of measure , as the argument shows).

# Change of variable formulas for integrals in dimension 1

We record in this file versions of the general change of variables formula in integrals for
functions from  to . This makes it possible to replace the determinant of the Fréchet
derivative with the one-dimensional derivative.

We also give more specific versions of these theorems for monotone and antitone functions: this
makes it possible to drop the injectivity assumption of the general theorems, as the derivative
is zero on the set of non-injectivity, which means that it can be discarded.

See also  for versions of
the change of variables formula in dimension 1 for non-monotone functions, formulated with
the interval integral and with stronger requirements on the integrand.

version): if a function  is injective and differentiable on a measurable set , then the
Lebesgue integral of a function  on  coincides with the Lebesgue integral
of  on .

version): if a function  is injective and differentiable on a measurable set , then a
function  is integrable on  if and only if  is integrable on

# Locally integrable functions

A function is called *locally integrable* () if it is integrable
on a neighborhood of every point. More generally, it is *locally integrable on * if it is
locally integrable on a neighbourhood within  of any point of .

This file contains properties of locally integrable functions, and integrability results
on compact sets.

## Main statements

* : A continuous function is locally integrable.
* : A function which is continuous on  is locally
  integrable on .

A function  is *locally integrable on s*, for , if for every  there is
a neighbourhood of  within  on which  is integrable.

Note that this is, in general, strictly weaker than local integrability with respect to
. For example,  is locally integrable on  with
respect to the Lebesgue measure, but it is *not* locally integrable with respect to the
Lebesgue measure restricted to .

If a function is locally integrable on a compact set, then it is integrable on that set.

If a function  is locally integrable on a set  in a second countable topological space,
then there exist countably many open sets  covering  such that  is integrable on each
set .

If a function  is locally integrable on a set  in a second countable topological space,
then there exists a sequence of open sets  covering  such that  is integrable on each
set .

If  is locally closed (e.g. open or closed), then  is locally integrable on  iff it is
integrable on every compact subset contained in .

point. In particular, it is integrable on all compact sets,

closed.)

If  is closed, being locally integrable on  w.r.t.  is equivalent to being locally
integrable with respect to . For the one-way implication without assuming  closed,
see .

If a function is locally integrable, then it is integrable on any compact set.

If a function is locally integrable, then it is integrable on an open neighborhood of any
compact set.

If a function is locally integrable in a second countable topological space,
then there exists a sequence of open sets covering the space on which it is integrable.

# If an  space is complete, so is the target space

If an  space is complete and nontrivial, then the target space is complete.

Consider a nonzero function  in . Given a Cauchy sequence  in , form
 the Cauchy sequence  in . By completeness, it converges. Consider a subsequence
 which converges almost everywhere. As  is nonzero, we get some  such that 
 converges along this subsequence and . Then  converges along this subsequence, and
 therefore along all indices as it is Cauchy.

# Simple functions

A function  from a measurable space to any type is called *simple*, if every preimage 
is measurable, and the range is finite. In this file, we define simple functions and establish their
basic properties; and we construct a sequence of simple functions approximating an arbitrary Borel
measurable function .

The theorem  shows that in order to prove something for an arbitrary
measurable function into , it is sufficient to show that the property holds for (multiples of)
characteristic functions and is closed under addition and supremum of increasing sequences of
functions.

A function  from a measurable space to any type is called *simple*,
if every preimage  is measurable, and the range is finite. This structure bundles
a function with these properties.

# Strongly measurable and finitely strongly measurable functions

A function  is said to be almost everywhere strongly measurable if  is almost everywhere
equal to a strongly measurable function, i.e. the sequential limit of simple functions.
It is said to be almost everywhere finitely strongly measurable with respect to a measure 
if the supports of those simple functions have finite measure.

Almost everywhere strongly measurable functions form the largest class of functions that can be
integrated using the Bochner integral.

# Strongly measurable and finitely strongly measurable functions

A function  is said to be strongly measurable if  is the sequential limit of simple functions.
It is said to be finitely strongly measurable with respect to a measure  if the supports
of those simple functions have finite measure.

If the target space has a second countable topology, strongly measurable and measurable are
equivalent.

If the measure is sigma-finite, strongly measurable and finitely strongly measurable are equivalent.

The main property of finitely strongly measurable functions is
: there exists a measurable set  such that the
function is supported on  and  is sigma-finite. As a consequence, we can prove some
results for those functions as if the measure was sigma-finite.

We provide a solid API for strongly measurable functions, as a basis for the Bochner integral.

# Uniform tightness

This file contains the definitions for uniform tightness for a family of Lp functions.
It is used as a hypothesis to the version of Vitali's convergence theorem for Lp spaces
that works also for spaces of infinite measure. This version of Vitali's theorem
is also proved later in the file.

## Main definitions

* :
  A sequence of functions  is uniformly tight in  if for all , there
  exists some measurable set  with finite measure such that the Lp-norm of
   restricted to  is smaller than  for all .

## Main results

* : a finite sequence of Lp functions is uniformly
  tight.
* : a sequence of Lp functions which is uniformly
  integrable and uniformly tight converges in Lp if it converges almost everywhere.
* : Vitali convergence theorem:
  a sequence of Lp functions converges in Lp if and only if it is uniformly integrable,
  uniformly tight and converges in measure.

# Uniform integrability

This file contains the definitions for uniform integrability (both in the measure theory sense
as well as the probability theory sense). This file also contains the Vitali convergence theorem
which establishes a relation between uniform integrability, convergence in measure and
Lp convergence.

Uniform integrability plays a vital role in the theory of martingales and most notably is used to
formulate the martingale convergence theorem.

## Main definitions

* : uniform integrability in the measure theory sense.
  In particular, a sequence of functions  is uniformly integrable if for all , there
  exists some  such that for all sets  of smaller measure than , the Lp-norm of
   restricted to  is smaller than  for all .
* : uniform integrability in the probability theory sense.
  In particular, a sequence of measurable functions  is uniformly integrable in the
  probability theory sense if it is uniformly integrable in the measure theory sense and
  has uniformly bounded Lp-norm.

## Main results

* : a finite sequence of Lp functions is uniformly
  integrable.
* : a sequence of Lp functions which is uniformly
  integrable converges in Lp if they converge almost everywhere.
* : Vitali convergence theorem:
  a sequence of Lp functions converges in Lp if and only if it is uniformly integrable
  and converges in measure.

## Tags
uniformly integrable, uniformly absolutely continuous integral, Vitali convergence theorem

Uniform integrability in the measure theory sense.

A sequence of functions  is said to be uniformly integrable if for all , there exists
some  such that for all sets  with measure less than , the Lp-norm of 
restricted to  is less than .

Uniform integrability is also known as uniformly absolutely continuous integrals.

In probability theory, a family of measurable functions is uniformly integrable if it is
uniformly integrable in the measure theory sense and is uniformly bounded.

# A.e. stabilizer of a set

In this file we define the a.e. stabilizer of a set under a measure-preserving group action.

The a.e. stabilizer  of a set 
is the set of the elements  such that  is a.e.-invariant under .

For a measure-preserving group action, this set is a subgroup of .
If the set is null or conull, then this subgroup is the whole group.
The converse is true for an ergodic action and a null-measurable set.

## Implementation notes

We define the a.e. stabilizer as a bundled ,
thus we do not deal with monoid actions.

Also, many lemmas in this file are true for a *quasi-measure-preserving* action,
but we don't have the corresponding typeclass.

A.e. stabilizer of a set under a group action.

A.e. stabilizer of a set under an additive group action.

# Definitions about invariant measures

In this file we define typeclasses for measures invariant under (scalar) multiplication.

- 
  says that the measure  is invariant under scalar multiplication by ;
-  is the additive version of this typeclass;
- , 
  say that the measure  is invariant under multiplication on the left and on the right,
  respectively.
- , 
  are the additive versions of these typeclasses.

For basic facts about the first two typeclasses, see .
For facts about left/right-invariant measures, see .

## Implementation Notes

The / typeclasses and the left/right multiplication typeclasses
were defined by different people with different tastes,
so the former explicitly use measures of the preimages,
while the latter use .

If the left/right multiplication is measurable
(which is the case in most if not all interesting examples),
these definitions are equivalent.

The definitions that use 
imply that the left (resp., right) multiplication is .

A measure  is invariant under an additive action of  on  if for any
measurable set  and , the measure of its preimage under  is equal
to the measure of .

A measure  is invariant under a multiplicative action of  on  if for any
measurable set  and , the measure of its preimage under  is equal
to the measure of .

A measure  on a measurable additive group is left invariant
 if the measure of left translations of a set are equal to the measure of the set itself.

A measure  on a measurable group is left invariant
 if the measure of left translations of a set are equal to the measure of the set itself.

A measure  on a measurable additive group is right invariant
 if the measure of right translations of a set are equal to the measure of the set itself.

A measure  on a measurable group is right invariant
 if the measure of right translations of a set are equal to the measure of the set itself.

## Temporary design adaptations

* In the current version, we refer to the amenability of the action of a group on a measure space
  (e.g. in  and ), even though a definition of
  amenability has not yet been given in Mathlib.
  This is because there are different notions of amenability for groups and for group actions,
  and a Mathlib definition should be provided at the greatest level of generality, on which there
  has not yet been a general consensus.
  At the present moment,  corresponds to the existence of a -invariant finitely
  additive probability measure.

It's curious that in measure theory, measures can be pushed forward, while in geometry, volumes can
be pulled back. And yet here, we are describing a situation involving measures in a geometric way.

 measure in  is the same as the  measure of its pushforward in .

# Geometry of numbers

In this file we prove some of the fundamental theorems in the geometry of numbers, as studied by
Hermann Minkowski.

## Main results

* : Blichfeldt's principle, existence of two distinct
  points in a subgroup such that the translates of a set by these two points are not disjoint when
  the covolume of the subgroup is larger than the volume of the set.
* : Minkowski's theorem, existence of
  a non-zero lattice point inside a convex symmetric domain of large enough volume.

* Voronoi diagrams
* See Pete L. Clark, *Abstract Geometry of Numbers: Linear Forms* (arXiv)
  for some more ideas.

## References

* Pete L. Clark, *Geometry of Numbers with Applications to Number Theory* p.28

**Blichfeldt's Theorem**. If the volume of the set  is larger than the covolume of the
countable subgroup  of , then there exist two distinct points  such that 
and  are not disjoint.

The **Minkowski Convex Body Theorem**. If  is a convex symmetric domain of  whose volume
is large enough compared to the covolume of a lattice  of , then it contains a non-zero
lattice point of .

The **Minkowski Convex Body Theorem for compact domain**. If  is a convex compact symmetric
domain of  whose volume is large enough compared to the covolume of a lattice  of , then it
contains a non-zero lattice point of . Compared to

that  is compact and  is discrete but provides a weaker inequality rather than a strict
inequality.

# Bochner Integration on Groups

We develop properties of integrals with a group as domain.
This file contains properties about integrability and Bochner integration.

Translating a function by left-multiplication does not change its integral with respect to a
left-invariant measure.

Translating a function by left-addition does not change its integral with respect to a
     left-invariant measure.

Translating a function by right-multiplication does not change its integral with respect to a
right-invariant measure.

Translating a function by right-addition does not change its integral with respect to a
     right-invariant measure.

If some left-translate of a function negates it, then the integral of the function with respect
to a left-invariant measure is 0.

If some left-translate of a function negates it, then the integral of the function with
     respect to a left-invariant measure is 0.

If some right-translate of a function negates it, then the integral of the function with respect
to a right-invariant measure is 0.

If some right-translate of a function negates it, then the integral of the function with
     respect to a right-invariant measure is 0.

# Lebesgue Integration on Groups

We develop properties of integrals with a group as domain.
This file contains properties about Lebesgue integration.

The Lebesgue integral of a function with respect to an inverse invariant measure is
invariant under the change of variables x ↦ x⁻¹.

The Lebesgue integral of a function with respect to an inverse invariant measure is
invariant under the change of variables x ↦ -x.

Translating a function by left-multiplication does not change its Lebesgue integral
with respect to a left-invariant measure.

Translating a function by left-addition does not change its Lebesgue integral with
     respect to a left-invariant measure.

Translating a function by right-multiplication does not change its Lebesgue integral
with respect to a right-invariant measure.

Translating a function by right-addition does not change its Lebesgue integral with
     respect to a right-invariant measure.

For nonzero regular left invariant measures, the integral of a continuous nonnegative function
  is 0 iff  is 0.

For nonzero regular left invariant measures, the integral of a continuous nonnegative
     function  is 0 iff  is 0.

# Measures on Groups

We develop some properties of measures on (topological) groups

* We define properties on measures: measures that are left or right invariant w.r.t. multiplication.
* We define the measure  and show that it is right invariant iff
   is left invariant.
* We define a class , requiring that the measure  is left-invariant, finite
  on compact sets, and positive on open sets.

We also give analogues of all these notions in the additive world.

An alternative way to prove that  is left invariant under addition.

An alternative way to prove that  is right invariant under multiplication.

An alternative way to prove that  is right invariant under addition.

The image of a left invariant measure under a left action is left invariant, assuming that
the action preserves multiplication.

The image of a left invariant measure under a left additive action is left
invariant, assuming that the action preserves addition.

The image of a right invariant measure under a left action is right invariant, assuming that
the action preserves multiplication.

The image of a right invariant measure under a left additive action is right
invariant, assuming that the action preserves addition.

The image of a left invariant measure under right multiplication is left invariant.

The image of a left invariant measure under right addition is left invariant.

The image of a right invariant measure under left multiplication is right invariant.

The image of a right invariant measure under left addition is right invariant.

# Measure theory in the product of groups

In this file we show properties about measure theory in products of measurable groups
and properties of iterated integrals in measurable groups.

These lemmas show the uniqueness of left invariant measures on measurable groups, up to
scaling. In this file we follow the proof and refer to the book *Measure Theory* by Paul Halmos.

The idea of the proof is to use the translation invariance of measures to prove 
for two sets  and , where  is a constant that does not depend on . Let  and  be
the characteristic functions of  and .

preserves the measure , which means that

Applying this to  and to  gives , which is the uniqueness up to
scalar multiplication.

The proof in [Halmos] seems to contain an omission in §60 Th. A, see
.

Note that this theory only applies in measurable groups, i.e., when multiplication and inversion
are measurable. This is not the case in general in locally compact groups, or even in compact
groups, when the topology is not second-countable. For arguments along the same line, but using
continuous functions instead of measurable sets and working in the general locally compact
setting, see the file .

# Bochner integral

The Bochner integral extends the definition of the Lebesgue integral to functions that map from a
measure space into a Banach space (complete normed vector space). It is constructed here using
the L1 Bochner integral constructed in the file .

The Bochner integral is defined through the extension process described in the file

## Notes

Some tips on how to prove a proposition if the API for the Bochner integral is not enough so that
you need to unfold the definition of the Bochner integral and go back to simple functions.

One method is to use the theorem  in the file
 (or one of the related results, like
 for functions in ), which allows you to prove something for an arbitrary
integrable function.

Another method is using the following steps.
See  for a complicated example, which proves
that , with the first integral sign being the Bochner integral of a real-valued
function , and the second and third integral signs being integrals on -valued
functions (called ). The proof of 
is scattered in sections with the name .

Here are the usual steps of proving that a property , say , holds for all
functions :

1. First go to the  space.

   For example, if you see , that is the norm of
    in  space. Rewrite using .

2. Show that the set  is closed in  using .

3. Show that the property holds for all simple functions  in  space.

   Typically, you need to convert various notions to their  counterpart, using lemmas
   like .

4. Since simple functions are dense in ,
   Use  or  for this argument.

Monotone convergence theorem for real-valued functions and Bochner integrals

If a monotone sequence of functions has an upper bound and the sequence of integrals of these
functions tends to the integral of the upper bound, then the sequence of functions converges
almost everywhere to the upper bound.

use the corresponding lemma for

If an antitone sequence of functions has a lower bound and the sequence of integrals of these
functions tends to the integral of the lower bound, then the sequence of functions converges
almost everywhere to the lower bound.

# Continuous linear maps composed with integration

The goal of this file is to prove that integration commutes with continuous linear maps.
This holds for simple functions. The general result follows from the continuity of all involved
operations on the space . Note that composition by a continuous linear map on  is not just
the composition, as we are dealing with classes of functions, but it has already been defined
as . We take advantage of this construction here.

Note that the integrability hypothesis in the two lemmas below is necessary: consider the case
where , , and  is only integrable on the first component.

measure space into a Banach space (complete normed vector space). It is constructed here
for L1 functions by extending the integral on simple functions. See the file

and corresponding API.

# Vitali-Carathéodory theorem

Vitali-Carathéodory theorem asserts the following. Consider an integrable function  on
a space with a regular measure. Then there exists a function  such that 
everywhere,  is lower semicontinuous, and the integral of  is arbitrarily close to that of
. This theorem is proved in this file, as .

Symmetrically, there exists  which is upper semicontinuous, with integral arbitrarily close
to that of . It follows from the previous statement applied to . It is formalized under
the name .

The most classical version of Vitali-Carathéodory theorem only ensures a large inequality
. For applications to the fundamental theorem of calculus, though, the strict inequality
 is important. Therefore, we prove the stronger version with strict inequalities in this
file. There is a price to pay: we require that the measure is -finite, which is not necessary for
the classical Vitali-Carathéodory theorem. Since this is satisfied in all applications, this is
not a real problem.

## Sketch of proof

Decomposing  as the difference of its positive and negative parts, it suffices to show that a
positive function can be bounded from above by a lower semicontinuous function, and from below
by an upper semicontinuous function, with integrals close to that of .

For the bound from above, write  as a series  of simple functions.
Then, approximate  by a larger open set  with measure very close to that of  (this is
possible by regularity of the measure), and set . It is
lower semicontinuous as a series of lower semicontinuous functions, and its integral is arbitrarily
close to that of .

For the bound from below, use finitely many terms in the series, and approximate  from inside by
a closed set . Then  is bounded from above by , it is
upper semicontinuous as a finite sum of upper semicontinuous functions, and its integral is
arbitrarily close to that of .

The main pain point in the implementation is that one needs to jump between the spaces , ,
 and  (and be careful that addition is not well behaved on ), and between
 and .

We first show the bound from above for simple functions and the nonnegative integral
(this is the main nontrivial mathematical point), then deduce it for general nonnegative functions,
first for the nonnegative integral and then for the Bochner integral.

Then we follow the same steps for the lower bound.

Finally, we glue them together to obtain the main statement
.

## Related results

Are you looking for a result on approximation by continuous functions (not just semicontinuous)?
See result , in the file
.

## References

Rudin, *Real and Complex Analysis* (Theorem 2.24)

# Circle Averages

For a function  on the complex plane, this file introduces the definition
 as a shorthand for the average of  on the circle with center  and
radius , equipped with the rotation-invariant measure of total volume one. Like
, this notion exists as a convenience. It avoids notationally inconvenient
compositions of  with  and avoids the need to manually eliminate  every time
an average is computed.

Note: Like the interval average defined in ,
the  defined here is a purely measure-theoretic average. It should not be confused
with , which is the path integral over the circle path. The relevant integrability
property  is , as defined in
.

Implementation Note: Like , s are defined for negative radii. The theorem
 shows that the average is independent of the radius' sign.

### Definition

Define  as the average value of  on the circle with center  and radius
. This is a real notion, which should not be confused with the complex path integral notion
defined in  (integrating with respect to ).

If 'f' is *not* circle integrable, then the circle average is zero by definition.

If two functions agree on the circle, then their circle averages agree.

of the radius.

## Implementation notes

### Naming

In literature, the integral of a function or a 1-form along a path
is called “line integral”, “path integral”, “curve integral”, or “curvilinear integral”.

We use the name “curve integral” instead of other names for the following reasons:

- for many people whose mother tongue is not English,
  “line integral” sounds like an integral along a straight line;

- we reserve the name "path integral" for Feynman-style integrals over the space of paths.

### Usage of s for 1-forms

Similarly to the way  uses continuous linear maps
while higher order derivatives use continuous multilinear maps,
this file uses  instead of continuous alternating maps for 1-forms.

### Differentiability assumptions

The definitions in this file make sense if the path is a piecewise $C^1$ curve.
Poincaré lemma (formalization WIP, see #24019) implies that for a closed 1-form on an open set ,
the integral depends on the homotopy class of the path only,
thus we can define the integral along a continuous path
or an element of the fundamental groupoid of .

### Usage of an extra field

The definitions in this file deal with -linear 1-forms.
This allows us to avoid using 
in 
and a future formalization of Poincaré lemma.

The function  which appears in the definition of a curve integral.

This definition is used to factor out common parts of lemmas
about  and .

if the function  is integrable on .

The actual definition uses ,
because both interval integrals and derivatives expect globally defined functions.

defined as $\int_0^1 \omega(\gamma(t))(\gamma'(t))$.

The actual definition uses  which uses 
and ,
because calculus-related definitions in Mathlib expect globally defined functions as arguments.

curve integral is defined using Bochner integral,
thus it is defined as zero whenever the codomain is not a complete space.

# Poincaré lemma for 1-forms

In this file we prove Poincaré lemma for 1-forms for convex sets.
Namely, we show that a closed 1-form on a convex subset of a normed space is exact.

We also prove that the integrals of a closed 1-form
along 2 curves that are joined by a -smooth homotopy are equal.
In the future, this will allow us to prove Poincaré lemma for simply connected open sets
and, more generally, for simply connected locally convex sets.

## The Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem for the Bochner integral

**Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem** provides sufficient conditions under which almost
 everywhere convergence of a sequence of functions implies the convergence of their integrals.
 We could weaken the condition  to require  instead
 (i.e. not requiring that  is measurable), but in all applications proving integrability
 is easier.

Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem for filters with a countable basis

Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem for series.

Corollary of the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem: If a sequence of functions  is
(eventually) uniformly bounded by a constant and converges (eventually) pointwise to a
function , then the integrals of  with respect to a finite measure  converge
to the integral of .

## The Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem for interval integrals
As an application, we show continuity of parametric integrals.

Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem for filters with a countable basis

Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem for parametric interval integrals.

Interval integrals commute with countable sums, when the supremum norms are summable (a
special case of the dominated convergence theorem).

# Links between an integral and its "improper" version

In its current state, mathlib only knows how to talk about definite ("proper") integrals,
in the sense that it treats integrals over  the same as it treats integrals over
. For example, the integral over  is **not** defined to be the limit of
the integral over  as  tends to , which is known as an **improper integral**.

Indeed, the "proper" definition is stronger than the "improper" one. The usual counterexample
is , which has an improper integral over  but no definite integral.

Although definite integrals have better properties, they are hardly usable when it comes to
computing integrals on unbounded sets, which is much easier using limits. Thus, in this file,
we prove various ways of studying the proper integral by studying the improper one.

## Definitions

The main definition of this file is . It is a rather technical definition
whose sole purpose is generalizing and factoring proofs. Given an index type , a countably
generated filter  over , and an -indexed family  of subsets of a measurable space 
equipped with a measure , one should think of a hypothesis  as
a sufficient condition for being able to interpret  (if it exists) as the limit of 
in φ i, f x ∂μil

When using this definition with a measure restricted to a set , which happens fairly often, one
should not try too hard to use a  of subsets of , as it often makes proofs
more complicated than necessary. See for example the proof of
 where we use  as a
 w.r.t. , instead of using .

the cases  and  separately.

### Choice of the interval

We use integral over  instead of one of the other
three possible intervals with the same endpoints for two reasons:

* this way  holds whenever
   is integrable on each interval; in particular, it works even if the measure  has an atom
  at ; this rules out  and  intervals;
* with this definition for a probability measure , the integral  equals
  the difference $F_μ(b)-F_μ(a)$, where $F_μ(a)=μ(-∞, a]$ is the
  cumulative distribution function
  of .

### Integrability on an interval


A function  is called *interval integrable* with respect to a measure  on an unordered
interval  if it is integrable on both intervals  and . One of these
intervals is always empty, so this property is equivalent to  being integrable on
.

## Basic properties of interval integrability
- interval integrability is symmetric, reflexive, transitive
- monotonicity and strong measurability of the interval integral
- if  is interval integrable, so are its absolute value and norm
- arithmetic properties

We prove several versions of FTC-1, all in the  namespace. Many of them follow
the naming scheme .
They formulate FTC in terms of .
Let us explain the meaning of each part of the name:

*  means that the theorem is about strict differentiability, see  and
  ;
*  means that the theorem is about differentiability in both endpoints; incompatible with
  ;
*  means that the theorem is about one-sided derivatives, see below for details;
*  means that instead of continuity the theorem assumes that  has a finite limit
  almost surely as  tends to  and/or ;
*  or  mean that the theorem is about differentiability in the right (resp., left)
  endpoint.

We also reformulate these theorems in terms of . These theorems are named

name.

# The layer cake formula / Cavalieri's principle / tail probability formula

In this file we prove the following layer cake formula.

Consider a non-negative measurable function  on a measure space. Apply pointwise
to it an increasing absolutely continuous function  vanishing at the origin, with
derivative  on the positive real line (in other words,  a primitive of a non-negative
locally integrable function  on the positive real line). Then the integral of the result,
, can be written as the integral over the positive real line of the "tail measures" of 
(i.e., a function giving the measures of the sets on which  exceeds different positive real
values) weighted by . In probability theory contexts, the "tail measures" could be referred to
as "tail probabilities" of the random variable , or as values of the "complementary cumulative
distribution function" of the random variable . The terminology "tail probability formula" is
therefore occasionally used for the layer cake formula (or a standard application of it).

The essence of the (mathematical) proof is Fubini's theorem.

We also give the most common application of the layer cake formula -
a representation of the integral of a nonnegative function f:
$$∫ f(ω) \,∂μ(ω) = ∫ μ \{ω \mid f(ω) ≥ t\} \,dt$$

Variants of the formulas with measures of sets of the form  instead of
 are also included.

## Main results

* 
  and :
  The general layer cake formulas with Lebesgue integrals, written in terms of measures of
  sets of the forms  and , respectively.
*  and
  :
  The most common special cases of the layer cake formulas, stating that for a nonnegative
  function f we have $∫ f(ω) \,∂μ(ω) = ∫ μ \{ω \mid f(ω) ≥ t\} \,dt$ and
  $∫ f(ω) \,∂μ(ω) = ∫ μ \{ω \mid f(ω) > t\} \,dt$, respectively.
* :
  A Bochner integral version of the most common special case of the layer cake formulas, stating
  that for an integrable and a.e.-nonnegative function f we have
  $∫ f(ω) \,∂μ(ω) = ∫ μ \{ω \mid f(ω) > t\} \,dt$.

## See also

Another common application, a representation of the integral of a real power of a nonnegative
function, is given in .

# Monotone convergence theorem and addition of Lebesgue integrals

The monotone convergence theorem (aka Beppo Levi lemma) states that the Lebesgue integral and
supremum can be swapped for a pointwise monotone sequence of functions. This file first proves
several variants of this theorem, then uses it to show that the Lebesgue integral is additive
(assuming one of the functions is at least ) and respects multiplication by
a constant.

countable type.

**Fatou's lemma**, version with  functions.

of their sum. The other inequality needs one of these functions to be (a.e.-)measurable.

 and primed versions of these lemmas.

# Dominated convergence theorem

Lebesgue's dominated convergence theorem states that the limit and Lebesgue integral of
a sequence of (almost everywhere) measurable functions can be swapped if the functions are
pointwise dominated by a fixed function. This file provides a few variants of the result.

**Dominated convergence theorem** for nonnegative  functions.

**Dominated convergence theorem** for nonnegative  functions.

**Dominated convergence theorem** for filters with a countable basis and
AEMeasurable functions.

**Dominated convergence theorem** for filters with a countable basis.

If a monotone sequence of functions has an upper bound and the sequence of integrals of these
functions tends to the integral of the upper bound, then the sequence of functions converges
almost everywhere to the upper bound. Auxiliary version assuming moreover that the
functions in the sequence are ae measurable.

If a monotone sequence of functions has an upper bound and the sequence of integrals of these
functions tends to the integral of the upper bound, then the sequence of functions converges
almost everywhere to the upper bound.

If an antitone sequence of functions has a lower bound and the sequence of integrals of these
functions tends to the integral of the lower bound, then the sequence of functions converges
almost everywhere to the lower bound.

# Marginals of multivariate functions

In this file, we define a convenient way to compute integrals of multivariate functions, especially
if you want to write expressions where you integrate only over some of the variables that the
function depends on. This is common in induction arguments involving integrals of multivariate
functions.
This constructions allows working with iterated integrals and applying Tonelli's theorem
and Fubini's theorem, without using measurable equivalences by changing the representation of your
space (e.g. ).

## Main Definitions

* Assume that  is a product of measurable spaces with measures  on ,
   is a function and .
  Then  or  is the function that integrates 
  over all variables in . It returns a function that still takes the same variables as ,
  but is constant in the variables in . Mathematically, if ,
  then  is the expression
  $$
  \vec{x}\mapsto \int\!\!\cdots\!\!\int f(\vec{x}[\vec{y}])dy_{i_1}\cdots dy_{i_k}.
  $$
  where $\vec{x}[\vec{y}]$ is the vector $\vec{x}$ with $x_{i_j}$ replaced by $y_{i_j}$ for all
  $1 \le j \le k$.
  If  is the distribution of a random variable, this is the marginal distribution of all
  variables not in  (but not the most general notion, since we only consider product measures
  here).
  Note that the notation  is not a binder, and returns a function.

## Main Results

*  is the analogue of Tonelli's theorem for iterated integrals. It states that
  for measurable functions  and disjoint finsets  and  we have
  .

## Implementation notes

The function  can have an arbitrary product as its domain (even infinite products), but the
set  of integration variables is a . We are assuming that the function  is measurable
for most of this file. Note that asking whether it is  is not even well-posed,
since there is no well-behaved measure on the domain of .

# Integrals of continuous functions with respect to regular measures

When a measure is regular, one may express the measure of compact sets and of open sets
in terms of the integral of continuous functions equal to 1 on the compact set, or to 0 outside
of the open set respectively.

In a locally compact regular space with an inner regular measure, the measure of a compact
set  is the infimum of the integrals of compactly supported functions equal to  on .

Given an inner regular finite measure, the measure of an open set is the supremum of the
integrals of nonnegative continuous functions supported in this set and bounded by .

Corollary of the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem: If a sequence of functions  is
(eventually) uniformly bounded by a constant and converges (eventually) pointwise to a
function , then the integrals of  with respect to a finite measure  converge
to the integral of .

The  operation is measurable. This shows that the integrand of (the right-hand-side
of) Fubini's theorem is measurable. This version has  in curried form.

# Measurable spaces and measurable functions

This file provides properties of measurable spaces and the functions and isomorphisms between them.
The definition of a measurable space is in .

A measurable space is a set equipped with a σ-algebra, a collection of subsets closed under
complementation and countable union. A function between measurable spaces is measurable if
the preimage of each measurable subset is measurable.

σ-algebras on a fixed set  form a complete lattice. Here we order σ-algebras by writing 
if every set which is -measurable is also -measurable (that is,  is a subset of ).
In particular, any collection of subsets of  generates a smallest σ-algebra which contains
all of them. A function  induces a Galois connection between the lattices of σ-algebras
on  and .

## Implementation notes

Measurability of a function  between measurable spaces is defined in terms of the
Galois connection induced by .

# Cardinal of sigma-algebras

If a sigma-algebra is generated by a set of sets , then the cardinality of the sigma-algebra is
bounded by . This is stated in 
and .

In particular, if , then the generated sigma-algebra has cardinality at most , see
.

For the proof, we rely on an explicit inductive construction of the sigma-algebra generated by
 (instead of the inductive predicate ). This transfinite inductive
construction is parameterized by an ordinal , and the cardinality bound is preserved along
each step of the construction. We show in  that this
indeed generates this sigma-algebra.

Transfinite induction construction of the sigma-algebra generated by a set of sets . At each
step, we add all elements of , the empty set, the complements of already constructed sets, and
countable unions of already constructed sets.

We index this construction by an arbitrary ordinal for simplicity, but by  we will have
generated all the sets in the sigma-algebra.

This construction is very similar to that of the Borel hierarchy.
